XVIII. BEOWULF AND GRENDEL’S MOTHER. 


P. 120, 1. 10. se. The poet gives Grendel’s mother sometimes a 
masculine (1. 141, 3. 246), sometimes a feminine (42. 253) pronoun, to 
show her giant and demon nature, which is that of a woman, because 
she has borne a son, but otherwise has more of the elemental strength 
and wildness of aman. (Heyne.) 

1. 19. agléca=Grendel. It may possibly mean Beowulf himself, 
as in p. 128, 1. 261. 

l. 22. him t6 anwaldan 4re gelifde ; literally, believed in mercy 
at (from) the Ruler for-himself, trusted in the Ruler’s mercy. It is not 
certain whether anwalda stands for gnwealda, or whether it should be 
read dnwealda =‘ only ruler.’ 

121, 1. 37. andweard may refer either to sweord (nom.) or swin 
(acc.); the former seems more probable, in which case it may be trans- 
lated ‘ face to face’ or ‘ with direct stroke,’ but the reading is altogether 
doubtful. 

1, 41. Se hine, he whom. 


202, NOTES. 


ll. 54, 5. Literally ‘the exchange was not good, which they had to 
pay for on both sides with the lives of their friends (or relations).’ 
These allusions to the Old Germanic system of establishing a definite 
pecuniary compensation for every injury, including loss of life, is com- 
mon in the poetry. Thus feohleds gefeoht (Bedw. 1. 2441) is a homicide 
which cannot be atoned for by money—in this case an unintentional 
fratricide. 

1. 62. eorla sum refers to Bedwulf—one of earls =one among earls, 
accompanied by earls (warriors). 

1. 64. wille. ‘The pret. wolde is more usual. 

122, 1. 82. eftsiSas, cognate acc.—proceeded on his return. 

1. 88. ealdres scyldig, having forfeited his life. 

1. 94. sed Se edw wel hwilcra wilna dohte: literally, which 
availed to-you of (for) nearly all joys=which was able to procure you 
every joy. 

123, 1. 100. gewitan. The ge may be merely a scribal error—a 
repetition (dittography) of the preceding ge of gewislicost. 

1. 113. hrinde bearwas. I have received a very satisfactory emenda- 
tion of this passage from Dr. R. Morris. He reads hrimge, ‘rimy,’ ‘ frosty,’ 
quoting a passage in one of the Blickling Homilies (St. Michael, p. 209), 
which is evidently a direct reminiscence of the description of the lake in 
Beowulf. ‘Swa sanctus Paulus wees gesednde on nordanweardne Sisne 
middangeard, Sé&r ealle weetero nider gewitad, and hé dér geseah ofer 
‘Sem weetere sumne hdrne stdin ; and weron nord of Sem stane Awe(a)xene 
swiSe hrimige bearwas, and S&r wéeron pystrogenipo, and under S&m 
stane wees nicra eardung and wearga. And hé geseah Szet on Seem clife 
hangodan on S&m isgean (read isgan) bearwum manige swearte saula be 
heora handum gebundne; and 84 fynd Sara on nicra onlicnesse heora 
gripende wéron, swa swa grédig wulf; and Set weeter wes sweart 
under Sm clife neoSan. And betuh Sm clife (read clifum) on Sem 
weetre wéron swylce twelf mila; and Sgnne 8A twigo forburston Sonne 
gewiton 6a saula nider, 84 Se on S&m twigum hangodon, and him 
gnféngon 84 nicras.’ Dr. Morris explains hrimge as‘ frosted with moss,’ 
but the synonym ésgan, ‘icy,’ shows that the word must be taken literally; 
he does not seem to have understood the latter word, for he writes és gean, 
and translates ‘ opposite.’ 

1. 120. seleS is not dependent on @r, for in that case it would be in 
the subjunctive, but ér is simply an adverb, correlative with the con- 
junction ér in the next line: ‘ he will (sooner) give up his life, before 
he will...’

is directly connected with wille: ‘ere he will hide his head in it,’ but 
this gives a much less forcible sense. |

XXIII. THE DREAM OF THE ROOD. 


P. 169, 1. 8. et foldan scedtum ; literally ‘at the surface of the 
earth’ =‘ at the foot of the Cross.’ 

1. 10. purh forSgesceaft, through the future, in eternity. 

170, 1. 26. Set, how. 

171, 1. 42. ymbclypte. The Old English idea of crucifixion was a 
very vague one, whence the inappropriate use of ymbelyppan here, and 
the general confusion of crucifixion with the gallows (I. 10) and hanging. 

1. 52. penian, passive. 

1. 58. t6 dnum, to him alone, in his solitude. 

172, 1. 62. eall ic wes mid strélum forwundod. This mention 

of ‘ arrows,’ of which there is no trace in the Bible narrative, is, as was 
first pointed out by Professor Stephens (‘The Ruthwell Cross; London, 
1866), a reminiscence of the myth of Baldor, the fairest and most 
beloved of the gods of Walhalla, who was slain by the mistletoe spear. 
The line ‘wedp eall gesceaft’ (1. 55) is also probably an allusion to 
Baldor’s death. 
' 1, 63. he&fdum. There are other examples of this peculiar use of 
the pl.; see Pastoral, 101. 16. It is probable that hedfod originally 
denoted a part of the head, perhaps the temples, and that the plural 
was used to denote the whole head. Bredst in the same way is generally 
used in the plural. See p. 178, 1. 113; Pastoral, 137. 8, 419. 29.

1. 66. of. Perhaps rather 9x =‘ in’

beorges sidan. If the reading banan be retained, in the sense of ‘mur-

XXV. SELECTIONS FROM THE RIDDLES OF 
CYNEWULF. 


P.179. The answers to these riddles are—(1) a cuckoo, (2) a horn 
(both drinking-horn and trumpet), (3) a Bible-codex, (4) the Moon and 
the Sun, (5) a book-worm, and (6) gnats. 

180, 1. 13. fredlic firdsceorp, asa... 

181, 1. 13. ford gew4&t. The context requires some such meaning 
as ‘came forth,’ ‘ approached.’ 

1. 16. m&re. There can be little doubt that the correct reading 
is méren (subj. of the vb. méran), suggested by Grein in his glossary. 
This makes the insertion of beoS unnecessary. 


XXVI. GNOMIC VERSES. 


P. 184, 1. 43. dirne creefte seems to be merely an adverbial peri- 
phrasis, ‘secretly,’ ‘ clandestinely.’ 

1. 45. bedgum gebyege, buy with rings (of gold), that is ‘seek in 
marriage.’ sealte. Perhaps better sealt (adj.) 

185, 1. 54. synne stélan ; lit. ‘ institute sin’ =‘ wage hostility,’ the 
ideas of ‘ sin,’ ‘injury,’ ‘ hostility,’ being conyertible. 

1, 60. bidad. Perhaps rather bidan. 


GLOSS A RY, 


ABBREVIATIONS. 


— sm., sf, sn., masculine (&c.) substantive. 


sv. strong verb. 
wy. weak verb. 


swu. strong-weak (preterito-present) verb. 
(The other abbreviations require no explanation.) 


ORDER. 


a 
The long vowels are separated from the short, and come immediately 


after them. 


ea and eo are considered as simple vowels, and follow e; and 


ed and ed follow ea and eo respectively. p follows ¢, and 8 follows d. 
Words with the prefix ge are placed in the order of their roots. Words 
beginning with ge are given apart from the simple word only when the ge 
is an integral part of the word, modifying its meaning. 
All words in -ing and -wng are given under -wng only, and all words in 


-nis and -nes under -mis only. 


Words enclosed in [ ] are cognate Old English words, or else Latin 


originals of foreign words.
